Author of the Gospel of Mark traditionally identified with John Mark</div>

<li><a href="/w/index.php?title=Parmouti_30_(Coptic_Orthodox_liturgics)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Parmouti 30 (Coptic Orthodox liturgics) (page does not exist)">30 Parmouti</a> or 8 May (<a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Orthodox">Coptic Orthodox</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Orthodox">Eastern Orthodox</a> <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Gregorian Calendar">Gregorian Calendar</a> date)</li></ul>
</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Barristers" class="mw-redirect" title="Barristers">Barristers</a>, <a href="/wiki/Venice" title="Venice">Venice</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Walsh,_p._21_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Walsh,_p._21-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Copts" title="Copts">Copts</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Mainar" title="Mainar">Mainar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Podgorica" title="Podgorica">Podgorica</a><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><span class="nowrap">Major works</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Mark" title="Gospel of Mark">Gospel of Mark</a> (attributed)</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Mark the Evangelist</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Marcus</i>; <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Μᾶρκος</span></span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek" title="Romanization of Greek">translit.</a></small>&#160;<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn"><i>Mârkos</i></i></span>; <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Aramaic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Imperial Aramaic language">Imperial Aramaic</a>: <span lang="arc" dir="rtl">ܡܪܩܘܣ</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Imperial Aramaic-language romanization"><i lang="arc-Latn">Marqōs</i></span>; <a href="/wiki/Ge%CA%BDez" title="Geʽez">Ge'ez</a>: ማርቆስ; <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a>: <span lang="he" dir="rtl">מארק</span>), also known as <b><a href="/wiki/John_Mark" title="John Mark">John Mark</a></b> or <b>Saint Mark</b>, is the person who is traditionally ascribed to be the author of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Mark" title="Gospel of Mark">Gospel of Mark</a>. Modern Bible scholars have concluded that the Gospel of Mark was written by an anonymous author rather than by Mark.<sup id="cite_ref-sanders_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sanders-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-lost_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lost-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-nickle_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nickle-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Carolina2004_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carolina2004-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> According to Church tradition, Mark founded the <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_see" title="Episcopal see">episcopal see</a> of <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Alexandria" title="Church of Alexandria">Alexandria</a>, which was one of the <a href="/wiki/Pentarchy" title="Pentarchy">five most important</a> sees of <a href="/wiki/Early_Christianity" title="Early Christianity">early Christianity</a>. His <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> is celebrated on April 25, and his <a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">symbol</a> is the <a href="/wiki/Lion_of_Saint_Mark" title="Lion of Saint Mark">winged lion</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-EECp720_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EECp720-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Mark.27s_identity"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Mark's_identity">Mark's identity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mark_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Mark&#039;s identity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Four_Evangelists" title="Four Evangelists">Four Evangelists</a></div>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Leone_marciano_andante_-_Vittore_Carpaccio_-_Google_Cultural_Institute.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Leone_marciano_andante_-_Vittore_Carpaccio_-_Google_Cultural_Institute.jpg/330px-Leone_marciano_andante_-_Vittore_Carpaccio_-_Google_Cultural_Institute.jpg" decoding="async" width="330" height="122" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Leone_marciano_andante_-_Vittore_Carpaccio_-_Google_Cultural_Institute.jpg/495px-Leone_marciano_andante_-_Vittore_Carpaccio_-_Google_Cultural_Institute.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Leone_marciano_andante_-_Vittore_Carpaccio_-_Google_Cultural_Institute.jpg/660px-Leone_marciano_andante_-_Vittore_Carpaccio_-_Google_Cultural_Institute.jpg 2x" data-file-width="9424" data-file-height="3490" /></a><figcaption>Mark the Evangelist's <a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">symbol</a> is the <a href="/wiki/Winged_lion" title="Winged lion">winged lion</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Lion_of_Saint_Mark" title="Lion of Saint Mark">Lion of Saint Mark</a>. Inscription: <i>PAX TIBI MARCE EVANGELISTA MEVS</i> ("peace be upon you, Mark, my evangelist"). The same lion is also the symbol of <a href="/wiki/Venice" title="Venice">Venice</a> (on illustration).</figcaption></figure>
<p>According to <a href="/wiki/William_L._Lane" title="William L. Lane">William Lane</a> (1974), an "unbroken tradition" identifies Mark the Evangelist with <a href="/wiki/John_Mark" title="John Mark">John Mark</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> and John Mark as the cousin of <a href="/wiki/Barnabas" title="Barnabas">Barnabas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> However, <a href="/wiki/Hippolytus_of_Rome" title="Hippolytus of Rome">Hippolytus of Rome</a> in <i>On the Seventy Apostles</i> distinguishes Mark the Evangelist (<a href="/wiki/2_Timothy_4" title="2 Timothy 4">2 Timothy 4</a>:11), John Mark (<a href="/wiki/Acts_12" title="Acts 12">Acts 12</a>:12, 25; 13:5, 13; 15:37), and <a href="/wiki/Mark_the_cousin_of_Barnabas" title="Mark the cousin of Barnabas">Mark the cousin of Barnabas</a> (<a href="/wiki/Colossians_4" title="Colossians 4">Colossians 4</a>:10; <a href="/wiki/Philemon_1" class="mw-redirect" title="Philemon 1">Philemon 1</a>:24).<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> According to Hippolytus, they all belonged to the "Seventy Disciples" who were sent out by <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> to disseminate the <a href="/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel">gospel</a> (<a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Luke</a> 10:1ff.) in <a href="/wiki/Judea" title="Judea">Judea</a>.
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Caesarea" class="mw-redirect" title="Eusebius of Caesarea">Eusebius of Caesarea</a> (<i>Eccl. Hist.</i> 2.9.1–4), <a href="/wiki/Herod_Agrippa_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Herod Agrippa I">Herod Agrippa I</a>, in his first year of reign over the whole of Judea (AD 41), killed <a href="/wiki/James,_son_of_Zebedee" class="mw-redirect" title="James, son of Zebedee">James, son of Zebedee</a> and arrested <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Peter</a>, planning to kill him after the <a href="/wiki/Passover" title="Passover">Passover</a>. Peter was saved miraculously by <a href="/wiki/Angel" title="Angel">angels</a>, and escaped out of the realm of Herod (Acts 12:1–19). Peter went to <a href="/wiki/Antioch" title="Antioch">Antioch</a>, then through <a href="/wiki/Asia_Minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Asia Minor">Asia Minor</a> (visiting the churches in <a href="/wiki/Pontus_(region)" title="Pontus (region)">Pontus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Galatia" title="Galatia">Galatia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cappadocia" title="Cappadocia">Cappadocia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Asia_(Roman_province)" title="Asia (Roman province)">Asia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Bithynia" title="Bithynia">Bithynia</a>, as mentioned in <a href="/wiki/1_Peter_1" title="1 Peter 1">1 Peter 1</a>:1), and arrived in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> in the second year of <a href="/wiki/Emperor_Claudius" class="mw-redirect" title="Emperor Claudius">Emperor Claudius</a> (AD 42; Eusebius, Eccl, Hist. 2.14.6). Somewhere on the way, Peter encountered Mark and took him as travel companion and interpreter. Mark the Evangelist wrote down the <a href="/wiki/Sermon" title="Sermon">sermons</a> of Peter, thus composing the Gospel according to Mark (<i>Eccl. Hist.</i> 15–16), before he left for <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a> in the third year of Claudius (AD 43).<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the Acts 15:39, Mark went to Cyprus with Barnabas after the Council of Jerusalem.
</p><p>According to tradition, in AD 49, about 19 years after the <a href="/wiki/Ascension_of_Jesus" title="Ascension of Jesus">Ascension of Jesus</a>, Mark travelled to <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a> and founded the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Alexandria" title="Church of Alexandria">Church of Alexandria</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church" title="Coptic Orthodox Church">Coptic Orthodox Church</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek Orthodox Church of Alexandria">Greek Orthodox Church of Alexandria</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Catholic_Church" title="Coptic Catholic Church">Coptic Catholic Church</a> all trace their origins to this original community.<sup id="cite_ref-georgetown1_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-georgetown1-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Aspects of the Coptic liturgy can be traced back to Mark himself.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> He became the first <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a> of Alexandria and he is honored as the founder of <a href="/wiki/Christianity_in_Africa" title="Christianity in Africa">Christianity in Africa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-OSVESp401_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OSVESp401-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Eusebius (<i>Eccl. Hist.</i> 2.24.1), Mark was succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Anianus_of_Alexandria" title="Pope Anianus of Alexandria">Anianus</a> as the bishop of Alexandria in the eighth year of <a href="/wiki/Nero" title="Nero">Nero</a> (62/63), probably, but not definitely, due to his coming death. Later Coptic tradition says that he was martyred in 68.<sup id="cite_ref-cocn_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cocn-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-macrory_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-macrory-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Modern Bible scholars (i.e. most critical scholars) have concluded that the Gospel of Mark was written by an anonymous author rather than by Mark.<sup id="cite_ref-Ehrman_2004_p._21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ehrman_2004_p.-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Staff_2012_p._344_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Staff_2012_p._344-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-B&amp;H_Publishing_Group_2006_p._1454_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-B&amp;H_Publishing_Group_2006_p._1454-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Easley_2002_p._233_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Easley_2002_p._233-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Craig_Lüdemann_Copan_Tacelli_2000_p._43_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Craig_Lüdemann_Copan_Tacelli_2000_p._43-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Jeon_Baugh_2017_p._181_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeon_Baugh_2017_p._181-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-sanders_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sanders-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-lost_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-lost-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-nickle_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nickle-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Carolina2004_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carolina2004-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ehrman_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ehrman-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ehrman_2006_p._143_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ehrman_2006_p._143-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ehrman_2000_p._55_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ehrman_2000_p._55-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Boring_2012_p._522_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Boring_2012_p._522-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ray_2018_p._123_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ray_2018_p._123-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Foster_2012_p._176_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Foster_2012_p._176-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> For instance, the author of the Gospel of Mark knew very little about the geography of <a href="/wiki/Palestine_(region)" title="Palestine (region)">Palestine</a> (he apparently never visited it),<sup id="cite_ref-leach_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leach-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Evans_2014_p._252_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Evans_2014_p._252-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReddish201136_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReddish201136-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> "was very far from being a peasant or a fisherman",<sup id="cite_ref-leach_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leach-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> was unacquainted with Jewish customs (i.e. from Palestine),<sup id="cite_ref-Evans_2014_p._252_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Evans_2014_p._252-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReddish201136_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReddish201136-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> and was probably "a Hellenized Jew who lived outside of Palestine".<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Mitchell Reddish does concede that the name of the author might have been Mark (making the gospel possibly homonymous), but the identity of this Mark is unknown.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReddish201136_37-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReddish201136-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Similarly, "Francis Moloney suggests the author was someone named Mark, though maybe not any of the Marks mentioned in the New Testament".<sup id="cite_ref-Tucker_Kuecker_2020_p._70_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tucker_Kuecker_2020_p._70-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> <i>The Routledge Encyclopedia of the Historical Jesus</i> takes the same approach: he was named Mark, but scholars are undecided who this Mark was.<sup id="cite_ref-Evans_2014_p._252_36-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Evans_2014_p._252-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The four canonical gospels are anonymous and most researchers agree that none of them was written by eyewitnesses.<sup id="cite_ref-Millard_2006_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Millard_2006-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReddish201113,_42_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReddish201113,_42-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECousland20101744_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECousland20101744-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECousland20181380_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECousland20181380-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Some conservative researchers defend their traditional authorship, but for a variety of reasons most scholars have abandoned this theory or support it only tenuously.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELindarsEdwardsCourt200041_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELindarsEdwardsCourt200041-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biblical_and_traditional_information">Biblical and traditional information</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mark_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Biblical and traditional information">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Evidence for Mark the Evangelist's authorship of the Gospel that bears his name originates with <a href="/wiki/Papias_of_Hierapolis" title="Papias of Hierapolis">Papias</a> (c. 60 – c. 130 AD).<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Papias01_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Papias01-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-NJBCp596_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NJBCp596-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Scholars of the <a href="/wiki/Trinity_Evangelical_Divinity_School" title="Trinity Evangelical Divinity School">Trinity Evangelical Divinity School</a> are "almost certain" that Papias is referencing <a href="/wiki/John_Mark" title="John Mark">John Mark</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Modern mainstream Bible scholars find Papias's information difficult to interpret.<sup id="cite_ref-MuddimanBarton2010_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MuddimanBarton2010-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria">Coptic Church</a> accords with identifying Mark the Evangelist with <a href="/wiki/John_Mark" title="John Mark">John Mark</a>, as well as that he was one of the Seventy Disciples sent out by Christ (Luke 10:1), as <a href="/wiki/Hippolytus_of_Rome" title="Hippolytus of Rome">Hippolytus</a> confirmed.<sup id="cite_ref-Shen01_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Shen01-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Coptic tradition also holds that Mark the Evangelist hosted the disciples in his house after Jesus's death, that the resurrected Jesus Christ came to Mark's house (<a href="/wiki/John_20" title="John 20">John 20</a>), and that the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Spirit" title="Holy Spirit">Holy Spirit</a> descended on the disciples at <a href="/wiki/Pentecost" title="Pentecost">Pentecost</a> in the same house.<sup id="cite_ref-Shen01_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Shen01-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Furthermore, Mark is also believed to have been among the servants at the <a href="/wiki/Marriage_at_Cana" class="mw-redirect" title="Marriage at Cana">Marriage at Cana</a> who poured out the water that Jesus turned to wine (<a href="/wiki/John_2" title="John 2">John 2</a>:1–11).<sup id="cite_ref-Shen01_50-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Shen01-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the Coptic tradition, Mark was born in <a href="/wiki/Cyrene,_Libya" title="Cyrene, Libya">Cyrene</a>, a city in the <a href="/wiki/Pentapolis_(North_Africa)" class="mw-redirect" title="Pentapolis (North Africa)">Pentapolis of North Africa</a> (now <a href="/wiki/Libya" title="Libya">Libya</a>). This tradition adds that Mark returned to Pentapolis later in life, after being sent by <a href="/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle" title="Paul the Apostle">Paul</a> to <a href="/wiki/Colossae" title="Colossae">Colossae</a> (<a href="/wiki/Colossians" class="mw-redirect" title="Colossians">Colossians</a> 4:10; <a href="/wiki/Epistle_to_Philemon" title="Epistle to Philemon">Philemon</a> 24. Some, however, think these actually refer to <a href="/wiki/Mark_the_Cousin_of_Barnabas" class="mw-redirect" title="Mark the Cousin of Barnabas">Mark the Cousin of Barnabas</a>), and serving with him in Rome (2 Tim 4:11); from Pentapolis he made his way to <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-SUSCopt_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SUSCopt-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>  When Mark returned to Alexandria, the pagans of the city resented his efforts to turn the Alexandrians away from the worship of their <a href="/wiki/Hellenistic_religion" title="Hellenistic religion">traditional gods</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Shen07_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Shen07-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> In AD 68, they placed a rope around his neck and dragged him through the streets until he was dead.<sup id="cite_ref-Shen07_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Shen07-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mark_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Saint_Mark%27s_relics" title="Saint Mark&#39;s relics">Saint Mark's relics</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:La_tradizione_del_dono_del_%22Bocol%22.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/La_tradizione_del_dono_del_%22Bocol%22.jpg/220px-La_tradizione_del_dono_del_%22Bocol%22.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="330" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/La_tradizione_del_dono_del_%22Bocol%22.jpg/330px-La_tradizione_del_dono_del_%22Bocol%22.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/La_tradizione_del_dono_del_%22Bocol%22.jpg/440px-La_tradizione_del_dono_del_%22Bocol%22.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="960" /></a><figcaption><i>Festa del bocoło</i> (<a href="/wiki/Rose" title="Rose">rosebud</a> festival) in <a href="/wiki/Piazza_San_Marco" title="Piazza San Marco">St Mark's Square</a>, Venice (Italy)</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Feast_of_St_Mark" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast of St Mark">Feast of St Mark</a> is observed on April 25 by the Catholic and Eastern Orthodox Churches. For those Churches still using the <a href="/wiki/Julian_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Julian Calendar">Julian Calendar</a>, April 25 according to it aligns with May 8 on the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Gregorian Calendar">Gregorian Calendar</a> until the year 2099. The Coptic Orthodox Church observes the Feast of St Mark on <a href="/w/index.php?title=Parmouti_30_(Coptic_Orthodox_liturgics)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Parmouti 30 (Coptic Orthodox liturgics) (page does not exist)">Parmouti 30</a> according to the <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Calendar">Coptic Calendar</a> which always aligns with April 25 on the <a href="/wiki/Julian_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Julian Calendar">Julian Calendar</a> or May 8 on the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_calendar" title="Gregorian calendar">Gregorian Calendar</a>.
</p><p>Where <a href="/wiki/John_Mark" title="John Mark">John Mark</a> is distinguished from Mark the Evangelist, John Mark is celebrated on September 27 (as in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Martyrology" title="Roman Martyrology">Roman Martyrology</a>) and Mark the Evangelist on April 25.
</p><p>Mark is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> and in much of the Anglican Communion, with a <a href="/wiki/Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Festival (Anglicanism)">Festival</a> on <a href="/wiki/April_25" title="April 25">25 April</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_art">In art</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Mark_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: In art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<p>Mark the Evangelist is most often depicted writing or holding his gospel.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> In Christian tradition, Mark the Evangelist is symbolized by a lion.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mark the Evangelist attributes are the lion in the <a href="/wiki/Desert" title="Desert">desert</a>; he can be depicted as a <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a> on a throne decorated with lions; as a man helping <a href="/wiki/Venice" title="Venice">Venetian</a> sailors. He is often depicted holding a book with <i>pax tibi Marce</i> written on it or holding a palm and book. Other depictions of Mark show him as a man with a book or scroll, accompanied by a winged lion. The lion might also be associated with Jesus' <a href="/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus" title="Resurrection of Jesus">Resurrection</a> because lions were believed to sleep with open eyes, thus a comparison with Christ in his tomb, and Christ as king.
</p><p>Mark the Evangelist can be depicted as a man with a halter around his neck and as rescuing Christian slaves from <a href="/wiki/Saracens" class="mw-redirect" title="Saracens">Saracens</a>.
</p>

<li id="cite_note-sanders-5"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-sanders_5-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-sanders_5-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFE._P._Sanders1995" class="citation book cs1">E. P. Sanders (30 November 1995). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=lkbTL36ZgPIC&amp;pg=PT103"><i>The Historical Figure of Jesus</i></a>. Penguin Books Limited. p.&#160;103. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-14-192822-7" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-14-192822-7"><bdi>978-0-14-192822-7</bdi></a>. <q>We do not know who wrote the gospels. They presently have headings: 'according to Matthew', 'according to Mark', 'according to Luke' and 'according to John'. The Matthew and John who are meant were two of the original disciples of Jesus. Mark was a follower of Paul, and possibly also of Peter; Luke was one of Paul's converts.<sup>5</sup> These men – Matthew, Mark, Luke and John – really lived, but we do not know that they wrote gospels. Present evidence indicates that the gospels remained untitled until the second half of the second century.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Historical+Figure+of+Jesus&amp;rft.pages=103&amp;rft.pub=Penguin+Books+Limited&amp;rft.date=1995-11-30&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-14-192822-7&amp;rft.au=E.+P.+Sanders&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DlkbTL36ZgPIC%26pg%3DPT103&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-lost-6"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-lost_6-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-lost_6-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFEhrman2005" class="citation book cs1"><a href="/wiki/Bart_D._Ehrman" title="Bart D. Ehrman">Ehrman, Bart D.</a> (2005). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=URdACxKubDIC&amp;pg=PA235"><i>Lost Christianities: The Battles for Scripture and the Faiths We Never Knew</i></a>. Oxford University Press. p.&#160;235. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-19-518249-1" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-19-518249-1"><bdi>978-0-19-518249-1</bdi></a>. <q>Why then do we call them Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John? Because sometime in the second century, when proto-orthodox Christians recognized the need for apostolic authorities, they attributed these books to apostles (Matthew and John) and close companions of apostles (Mark, the secretary of Peter; and Luke, the traveling companion of Paul). Most scholars today have abandoned these identifications,<sup>11</sup> and recognize that the books were written by otherwise unknown but relatively well-educated Greek-speaking (and writing) Christians during the second half of the first century.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Lost+Christianities%3A+The+Battles+for+Scripture+and+the+Faiths+We+Never+Knew&amp;rft.pages=235&amp;rft.pub=Oxford+University+Press&amp;rft.date=2005&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-19-518249-1&amp;rft.aulast=Ehrman&amp;rft.aufirst=Bart+D.&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DURdACxKubDIC%26pg%3DPA235&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-nickle-7"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-nickle_7-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-nickle_7-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFNickle2001" class="citation book cs1">Nickle, Keith Fullerton (January 1, 2001). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=5SSytjasmAgC&amp;pg=PA43"><i>The Synoptic Gospels: An Introduction</i></a>. Westminster John Knox Press. p.&#160;43. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-664-22349-6" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-664-22349-6"><bdi>978-0-664-22349-6</bdi></a>. <q>We must candidly acknowledge that all three of the Synoptic Gospels are anonymous documents. None of the three gains any importance by association with those traditional figures out of the life of the early church. Neither do they lose anything in importance by being recognized to be anonymous. Throughout this book the traditional names are used to refer to the authors of the first three Gospels, but we shall do so simply as a device of convenience.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Synoptic+Gospels%3A+An+Introduction&amp;rft.pages=43&amp;rft.pub=Westminster+John+Knox+Press&amp;rft.date=2001-01-01&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-664-22349-6&amp;rft.aulast=Nickle&amp;rft.aufirst=Keith+Fullerton&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3D5SSytjasmAgC%26pg%3DPA43&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Carolina2004-8"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-Carolina2004_8-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Carolina2004_8-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFEhrman2004" class="citation book cs1"><a href="/wiki/Bart_D._Ehrman" title="Bart D. Ehrman">Ehrman, Bart D.</a> (November 1, 2004). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=vbLK6kn5T-EC&amp;pg=PA110"><i>Truth and Fiction in The Da Vinci Code&#160;: A Historian Reveals What We Really Know about Jesus, Mary Magdalene, and Constantine</i></a>. Oxford University Press, USA. pp.&#160;110–111. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-19-534616-9" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-19-534616-9"><bdi>978-0-19-534616-9</bdi></a>. <q>We call these books, of course, Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John. And for centuries Christians have believed they were actually written by these people: two of the disciples of Jesus, Matthew the tax collector (see Matt. 9:9) and John, the "beloved disciple" (John 21:24), and two companions of the apostles, Mark, the secretary of Peter, and Luke, the traveling companion of Paul. These are, after all, the names found in the titles of these books. But what most people don't realize is that these titles were added later, by second-century Christians, decades after the books themselves had been written, in order to be able to claim that they were apostolic in origin. Why would later Christians do this? Recall our earlier discussion of the formation of the New Testament canon: only those books that were apostolic could be included. What was one to do with Gospels that were widely read and accepted as authoritative but that in fact were written anonymously, as all four of the New Testament Gospels were? They had to be associated with apostles in order to be included in the canon, and so apostolic names were attached to them.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Truth+and+Fiction+in+The+Da+Vinci+Code+%3A+A+Historian+Reveals+What+We+Really+Know+about+Jesus%2C+Mary+Magdalene%2C+and+Constantine&amp;rft.pages=110-111&amp;rft.pub=Oxford+University+Press%2C+USA&amp;rft.date=2004-11-01&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-19-534616-9&amp;rft.aulast=Ehrman&amp;rft.aufirst=Bart+D.&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DvbLK6kn5T-EC%26pg%3DPA110&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>

(1998), "Mark", in Ferguson, Everett (ed.), <i>Encyclopedia of Early Christianity</i> (2nd&#160;ed.), New York and London: Garland Publishing, Inc., p.&#160;720, <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-8153-3319-6" title="Special:BookSources/0-8153-3319-6"><bdi>0-8153-3319-6</bdi></a></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=Mark&amp;rft.btitle=Encyclopedia+of+Early+Christianity&amp;rft.place=New+York+and+London&amp;rft.pages=720&amp;rft.edition=2nd&amp;rft.pub=Garland+Publishing%2C+Inc.&amp;rft.date=1998&amp;rft.isbn=0-8153-3319-6&amp;rft.aulast=Senior&amp;rft.aufirst=Donald+P.&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-Ehrman_2006_p._143-28"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Ehrman_2006_p._143_28-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFEhrman2006" class="citation book cs1">Ehrman, Bart D. (2006). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=oBuJMhJlTYwC&amp;pg=PA143"><i>The Lost Gospel of Judas Iscariot: A New Look at Betrayer and Betrayed</i></a>. Oxford University Press. p.&#160;143. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-19-971104-8" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-19-971104-8"><bdi>978-0-19-971104-8</bdi></a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">13 August</span> 2023</span>. <q>The Gospels of the New Testament are therefore our earliest accounts. These do not claim to be written by eyewitnesses to the life of Jesus, and historians have long recognized that they were produced by second- or third-generation Christians living in different countries than Jesus  (and Judas) did, speaking a different language (Greek instead of  Aramaic), experiencing different situations, and addressing different audiences.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Lost+Gospel+of+Judas+Iscariot%3A+A+New+Look+at+Betrayer+and+Betrayed&amp;rft.pages=143&amp;rft.pub=Oxford+University+Press&amp;rft.date=2006&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-19-971104-8&amp;rft.aulast=Ehrman&amp;rft.aufirst=Bart+D.&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DoBuJMhJlTYwC%26pg%3DPA143&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-Ehrman_2000_p._55-29"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Ehrman_2000_p._55_29-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFEhrman2000" class="citation book cs1">Ehrman, Bart D. (2000). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=kF8RAQAAIAAJ"><i>The New Testament: A Historical Introduction to the Early Christian Writings</i></a>. Oxford University Press. p.&#160;55. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-19-512639-6" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-19-512639-6"><bdi>978-0-19-512639-6</bdi></a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">13 August</span> 2023</span>. <q>We have already learned significant bits of information about these books. They were written thirty-five to sixty-five years after Jesus' death by authors who did not know him, authors living in different countries who were writing at different times to different communities with different problems and concerns. The authors all wrote in Greek and they all used sources for the stories they narrate. Luke explicitly indicates that his sources were both written and oral. These sources appear to have recounted the words and deeds of Jesus that had been circulating among Christian congregations throughout the Mediterranean world. At a later stage we will consider the question of the historical reliability of these stories. Here we are interested in the Gospels as pieces of early Christian literature.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+New+Testament%3A+A+Historical+Introduction+to+the+Early+Christian+Writings&amp;rft.pages=55&amp;rft.pub=Oxford+University+Press&amp;rft.date=2000&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-19-512639-6&amp;rft.aulast=Ehrman&amp;rft.aufirst=Bart+D.&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DkF8RAQAAIAAJ&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-Boring_2012_p._522-30"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Boring_2012_p._522_30-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFBoring2012" class="citation book cs1">Boring, M. Eugene (2012). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=leFrgkyEtusC&amp;pg=PA522"><i>An Introduction to the New Testament: History, Literature, Theology</i></a>. Presbyterian Publishing Corporation. p.&#160;522. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-664-25592-3" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-664-25592-3"><bdi>978-0-664-25592-3</bdi></a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">13 August</span> 2023</span>. <q>Beginning with Papias in the second century, a tradition developed in various forms that attributed the authorship of the Gospel of Mark to this John Mark, who had been the companion of both Paul and Peter (Eusebius, <i>Hist. eccl.</i> 3.39.15). In all its variations, the ancient tradition makes clear that Mark's Gospel was accepted and valued in the church, not because of its historical accuracy, but because it represented Peter's apostolic authority. The Gospel of Mark itself makes no claim to have been written by an eyewitness and gives no evidence of such authorship. While most critical scholars consider the actual author's name to be unknown, the traditional view that Mark was written in Rome by a companion of Peter is still defended by some scholars who begin with the church tradition cited above and do not find convincing historical evidence to disprove it.<sup>6</sup> For convenience, in this book we continue to refer to the Gospels by the names of their traditional authors.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=An+Introduction+to+the+New+Testament%3A+History%2C+Literature%2C+Theology&amp;rft.pages=522&amp;rft.pub=Presbyterian+Publishing+Corporation&amp;rft.date=2012&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-664-25592-3&amp;rft.aulast=Boring&amp;rft.aufirst=M.+Eugene&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DleFrgkyEtusC%26pg%3DPA522&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Ray_2018_p._123-31"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Ray_2018_p._123_31-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFRay2018" class="citation book cs1">Ray, Ronald R. (2018). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.nl/books?id=-j9qDwAAQBAJ&amp;pg=PA123"><i>Systematics Critical and Constructive 1: Biblical-Interpretive-Theological-Interdisciplinary</i></a>. Pickwick Publications. p.&#160;123. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-5326-0016-6" title="Special:BookSources/978-1-5326-0016-6"><bdi>978-1-5326-0016-6</bdi></a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">15 August</span> 2023</span>. <q>Authorship by an apostle was so unimportant to early recognition of a writing's authority that names of apostles (Matthew and John) or names of people thought to be associated with apostles (Mark and Luke respectively with Peter and Paul) were only attached to the four Gospels at the beginning of the second century, after those had gained recognition primarily because of churchly appreciation of their content. Having studied the content of John and Matthew, historical-critical scholarship massively doubts that the Hellenistic Fourth Gospel was authored by the apostle John, and widely doubts that the First Gospel was written by the apostle Matthew. That the author of Mark was Peter's associate also seems unlikely, since that Gospel is very Hellenistic and Peter—according to both Acts and Paul—was highly Jewish. Similarly, that the author of Luke was Paul's companion is most improbable, since Acts's accounts concerning Paul conflict much with what Paul's epistles report. Again, had any of the Gospels been written by apostles, why were their names attached so late?<sup>125</sup> Nor would apostle associates have been apostles!</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Systematics+Critical+and+Constructive+1%3A+Biblical-Interpretive-Theological-Interdisciplinary&amp;rft.pages=123&amp;rft.pub=Pickwick+Publications&amp;rft.date=2018&amp;rft.isbn=978-1-5326-0016-6&amp;rft.aulast=Ray&amp;rft.aufirst=Ronald+R.&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.nl%2Fbooks%3Fid%3D-j9qDwAAQBAJ%26pg%3DPA123&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Foster_2012_p._176-32"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Foster_2012_p._176_32-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Which is not a new claim, see <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFFoster2012" class="citation book cs1">Foster, Douglas A. (2012). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.nl/books?id=gv-AEAAAQBAJ&amp;pg=PT176"><i>The Encyclopedia of the Stone-Campbell Movement</i></a>. Wm. B. Eerdmans Publishing Company. p.&#160;176. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-4674-2736-4" title="Special:BookSources/978-1-4674-2736-4"><bdi>978-1-4674-2736-4</bdi></a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">15 August</span> 2023</span>. <q>During this period Disciples scholars such as Willett began to study at interdenominational theological schools and secular universities, and for the first time the Stone-Campbell Movement engaged historical criticism as the primary perspective on biblical interpretation. While Campbell's "Seven Rules" had advocated a kind of historical criticism, traditional conclusions about authorship, date, and the nature of biblical documents had been assumed, so that no one in the first generation had supposed that the consistent application of Campbell's own principles would lead to results that challenged and overturned these conclusions. By the end of the nineteenth century, those who followed the critical method arrived at a new set of conclusions that made the Bible look entirely different. Among these new conclusions: the Pentateuch was not written by Moses but represented a long development within history, the prophets were not making long-range predictions about Jesus and the church, but spoke to the issues of their own time; the Gospels were not independent 'testimonies" that provided "evidence" for the historical facts about Jesus' life and teaching, but were interdependent (Matthew and Luke used Mark and "Q"); also, the Gospels were not written by apostles and contained several layers of reinterpreted traditions.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Encyclopedia+of+the+Stone-Campbell+Movement&amp;rft.pages=176&amp;rft.pub=Wm.+B.+Eerdmans+Publishing+Company&amp;rft.date=2012&amp;rft.isbn=978-1-4674-2736-4&amp;rft.aulast=Foster&amp;rft.aufirst=Douglas+A.&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.nl%2Fbooks%3Fid%3Dgv-AEAAAQBAJ%26pg%3DPT176&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-Evans_2014_p._252-36"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-Evans_2014_p._252_36-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Evans_2014_p._252_36-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Evans_2014_p._252_36-2"><sup><i><b>c</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFHatina2014" class="citation book cs1">Hatina, Thomas R. (2014). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=StasAgAAQBAJ&amp;pg=PA252">"Gospel of Mark"</a>. In Evans, Craig A. (ed.). <i>The Routledge Encyclopedia of the Historical Jesus</i>. Taylor &amp; Francis. p.&#160;252. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-317-72224-3" title="Special:BookSources/978-1-317-72224-3"><bdi>978-1-317-72224-3</bdi></a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">13 August</span> 2023</span>. <q>Like the other synoptics, Mark's Gospel is anonymous. Whether it was originally so is, however, difficult to know. Nevertheless, we can be fairly certain that it was written by someone named Mark. [...] The difficulty is ascertaining the identity of Mark. Scholars debate [...] or another person simply named Mark who was not native to Palestine. Many scholars have opted for the latter option due to the Gospel's lack of understanding of Jewish laws (1:40-45; 2:23-28; 7:1-23), incorrect Palestinian geography (5:1-2, 12-13; 7:31), and concern for Gentiles (7:24-28:10) (e.g. Marcus 1999: 17-21).</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=Gospel+of+Mark&amp;rft.btitle=The+Routledge+Encyclopedia+of+the+Historical+Jesus&amp;rft.pages=252&amp;rft.pub=Taylor+%26+Francis&amp;rft.date=2014&amp;rft.isbn=978-1-317-72224-3&amp;rft.aulast=Hatina&amp;rft.aufirst=Thomas+R.&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DStasAgAAQBAJ%26pg%3DPA252&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEReddish201136-37"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReddish201136_37-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReddish201136_37-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReddish201136_37-2"><sup><i><b>c</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFReddish2011">Reddish 2011</a>, p.&#160;36: "Evidence in the Gospel itself has led many readers of the Gospel to question the traditional view of authorship. The author of the Gospel does not seem to be too familiar with Palestinian geography. [...] Is it likely that a native of Palestine, as John Mark was, would have made such errors?" [...] Also, certain passages in the Gospel contain erroneous statements about Palestinian or Jewish practices."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-38"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-38">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFWatts_Henderson2018" class="citation book cs1">Watts Henderson, Suzanne (2018). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=T05WDwAAQBAJ&amp;pg=PA1431">"The Gospel according to Mark"</a>. <li id="cite_note-Millard_2006-40"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Millard_2006_40-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFMillard2006" class="citation book cs1">Millard, Alan (2006). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=eKZYMifS1fAC&amp;dq=%22written+virtually+entirely+in+a+form+of+ancient+Greek%22&amp;pg=PA558">"Authors, Books, and Readers in the Ancient World"</a>. <li id="cite_note-MuddimanBarton2010-49"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-MuddimanBarton2010_49-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFWansbrough2010" class="citation book cs1">Wansbrough, Henry (22 April 2010). Muddiman, John; Barton, John (eds.). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=utMUDAAAQBAJ&amp;pg=PA243"><i>The Gospels</i></a>. Oxford University Press. p.&#160;243. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-19-958025-5" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-19-958025-5"><bdi>978-0-19-958025-5</bdi></a>. <q>Finally it is important to realize that none of the four gospels originally included an attribution to an author. All were anonymous, and it is only from the fragmentary and enigmatic and—according to Eusebius, from whom we derive the quotation—unreliable evidence of Papias in 120/130 CE that we can begin to piece together any external evidence about the names of their authors and their compilers. This evidence is so difficult to interpret that most modern scholars form their opinions from the content of the gospels themselves, and only then appeal selectively to the external evidence for confirmation of their findings.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Gospels&amp;rft.pages=243&amp;rft.pub=Oxford+University+Press&amp;rft.date=2010-04-22&amp;rft.isbn=978-0-19-958025-5&amp;rft.aulast=Wansbrough&amp;rft.aufirst=Henry&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DutMUDAAAQBAJ%26pg%3DPA243&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AMark+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>
